He agrees with Yudkowsky and Zvi that interpretability-in-the-loop training partially optimizes for obfuscated misbehavior and that this is very dangerous, but he thinks the human brain does interpretability-in-the-loop learning in a different way that avoids a core issue. Namely, he argues that the human brain learns beliefs based on prediction error for sensory inputs, but learns desires (which assign numerical scores to beliefs) based on innate reward functions (which are functions of our abstract ‘interpreted’ beliefs). If these interpretability-derived rewards were used to update our beliefs, our beliefs would be updated towards those beliefs that, when interpreted, produce high reward. But instead, only our desires are updated based on these rewards, and our beliefs continue to be grounded in and updated by their correspondence to reality. This may suggest technical approaches that can reduce the risk of interpretability-in-the-loop training. I don’t understand this very well, but I feel vaguely positive about this kind of approach. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ArXAyzHkidxwoeZsL/in-highly-contingent-defense-of-interpretability-in-the-loop?commentId=JAgrH4xEXgmhZbc6A"><span>One comment</span></a><span> (from ‘cloud’) suggested that gradient routing might allow us to implement something similar (if we can separate the neural net parameters responsible for beliefs and desires, which seems very hard).</span></p><h2><b><span>10. Overall, I think we should probably incorporate some (and maybe many) proxies into training</span></b></h2><p><span>My weak current guess is that we can and should train with proxies for alignment, and that there is more work to be done in improving the situation. Ideally, we would have several types of misbehavior detectors such that the easiest ways to learn to bypass one of them are easily caught by another that we’re training against and multiple others that we’re not training against. It would probably also be good to do targeted interventions for which we have good evidence or arguments for generalization (rather than just penalizing misbehavior), and to use some unsupervised alignment methods that don’t rely on misbehavior detection. Some types of misbehavior detectors we could incorporate into training:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Activation monitors, CoT monitors, output monitors</span></li><li value="2"><span>Monitors based on the model we're training and other models</span></li><li value="3"><span>Monitors with their weights fixed throughout training and monitors that get updated alongside the policy model</span></li><li value="4"><span>Monitors prompted to pay attention to different things, or using</span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/confessions/"><span> </span></a><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/confessions/"><span>confessions</span></a><span> or</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/og7km7vmJ6Ktay9Ds/split-personality-training-revealing-latent-knowledge-1"><span> </span></a><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/og7km7vmJ6Ktay9Ds/split-personality-training-revealing-latent-knowledge-1"><span>split personality training</span></a></li><li value="5"><span>Monitors that see all of the activations, CoT, and outputs rather than separate monitors for each</span></li></ul><p><span>It may be very important to hold out some of the misbehavior detectors solely for evaluation, but if there is enough diversity, it seems plausible to me that we can just train against them all and be much more likely to get good behavior than obfuscated misbehavior. Admittedly, this is a lot of different training signals and it seems complicated and maybe expensive to incorporate all of them.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreff78n07gbtor"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnf78n07gbtor">[6]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>I’ll quickly enumerate some of the ways I think I disagree with John Wentworth (and others who are similarly opposed to training with alignment proxies):</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>I think current training-based approaches to alignment are working moderately well, and we can probably avoid hard-to-detect scheming without dramatically changing our alignment training methods</span></li><li value="2"><span>I don’t think it’s very likely that the issues that emerge from training against imperfect proxies are likely to involve hard-to-detect catastrophic risks, rather than smaller problems that are still visible and fixable with other proxies</span></li><li value="3"><span>I think writing robust alignment into a neural net might be incompatible with continual learning (CL), because CL will constantly change the weights and distort the model internals in ways that break alignment</span></li><li value="4"><span>It seems quite intractable to learn how to write robust alignment into neural nets using deep understanding of model internals</span></li></ul><h2><b><span>11. There are several interesting research directions that could help us make better choices about the use of proxies in training and evaluation</span></b></h2><p><span>Here is a list of questions that can motivate some interesting experiments.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>What should we monitor for misbehavior during training?</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>CoT, internals, outputs, some subset of these (with the same monitor), multiple monitors at the same time, or something else?</span></li><li value="2"><span>What are the implications of doing alignment training with different timescales / causal orders?</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>What should we prompt a monitor to look for? What are all the possible judge prompts that can help alignment training?</span></li><li value="3"><span>What model(s) should we use as misbehavior detectors?</span></li><li value="4"><span>What subset of our available proxies should we use for training vs. evaluation?</span></li><li value="5"><span>Can we train against many diverse proxies such that models cannot easily subvert all of them at once and instead learn robust alignment?</span></li><li value="6"><span>What should we do when we detect slop, reward hacking, or scheming?</span></li><li value="7"><span>What does it mean to have conflicting training signals in the CoT and outputs? How does this depend on training order?</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3o552d03mjr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3o552d03mjr">[7]</a></sup></span></li></ul><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span>A more thorough (but slightly sloppy and AI-generated) list of proxies and degrees of freedom</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><div class="llm-content-block"><div class="llm-content-block-content"><p><span>Here's my most thorough current list of proxies:</span></p><p><span>Proxies for desired behavior:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Detectors for reward hacking, slop, scheming, and other types of misbehavior</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ol><li value="1"><span>Human/AI judge ratings of outputs (RLHF reward models, CAI HHH reward models, deliberative alignment judges)</span></li><li value="2"><span>Output monitors (judging the final code/answer)</span></li><li value="3"><span>CoT / reasoning monitors (judging the chain of thought, possibly plus output, possibly plus tool calls / full trajectory)</span></li><li value="4"><span>Activation-based / white-box misbehavior detectors (e.g., linear probes for lying/deception, non-linear probes, activation oracles, misuse probes, coup probes, SAE-based detectors)</span></li><li value="5"><span>Model's self-evaluation of its own trajectory (self-filter+discard, reasoning-in-hindsight, "let the AIs choose which data counts as high reward")</span></li><li value="6"><span>Long-timescale proxies: "whether the behavior and outcomes from an agent over months of real-world operation look aligned"</span></li><li value="7"><span>Confessions-based monitors and split-personality-training monitors</span></li></ol></li><li value="2"><span>Token-by-token "outputs we think are pretty good in this context" (SFT targets)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Responses generated by a more capable teacher model with the constitution in context vs. responses without it (Open Character Training DPO signal)</span></li><li value="4"><span>What the model says when instructed to embody a set of character traits in self-reflection and self-interaction (Open Character Training SFT signal)</span></li><li value="5"><span>Verifier output fed back to the model for self-distillation (reflection on "what hacks were used / how could I have satisfied intent better")</span></li></ol><p><span>Degrees of freedom for training against proxies:</span></p><p><span>A. </span><i><span>What the proxy observes</span></i><span> — activations vs. CoT vs. outputs vs. full trajectory; a single monitor that sees all of {activations, CoT, outputs} vs. separate monitors for each</span></p><p><span>B. </span><i><span>Timescale / causal-order position of the proxy</span></i><span> — mech-interp (sub-forward-pass) → CoT → final-answer → long-horizon real-world behavior</span></p><p><span>C. </span><i><span>What model serves as the monitor</span></i><span> — the policy model itself, another copy of it, a different model, or an ensemble</span></p><p><span>D. </span><i><span>Whether the monitor's weights are frozen or updated alongside the policy</span></i><span> (frozen probe vs. probe retrained every N steps)</span></p><p><span>E. </span><i><span>What the monitor is prompted to look for</span></i><span> — different judge prompts, confessions-style prompts, split-personality prompts</span></p><p><span>F. </span><i><span>Which subset of available proxies is used for training vs. held out for evaluation</span></i><span> (and how correlated the training proxies' failures are with the evaluation proxies' failures)</span></p><p><span>G. </span><i><span>Diversity and number of simultaneously-trained-against proxies</span></i><span> (single vs. multiple jointly, to make joint evasion hard)</span></p><p><span>H. </span><i><span>How strong / accurate the proxy is</span></i><span> (e.g., detector TPR threshold — FAR's results on weak vs. strong lie detectors)</span></p><p><span>I. </span><i><span>How the training signal is applied when misbehavior is detected</span></i><span> — chosen from the list of targeted interventions: fix the environment; self-filter+discard; external filter+discard; classification+penalty; offline filtering then distillation; inoculation prompting; persona steering; good→neutral recontextualization; deliberative alignment / RLAIF on extracted principles; self-distillation via verifier-output reflection</span></p><p><span>J. </span><i><span>RL algorithm / optimization strength</span></i><span> — on-policy RL (e.g., GRPO) vs. off-policy (e.g., DPO); strength of KL regularization to a reference/base model; penalty magnitude</span></p><p><span>K. </span><i><span>Validation vs. training use of the proxy</span></i><span> (MacDermott's distinction — resample-and-discard filter vs. gradient signal)</span></p><p><span>L. </span><i><span>Relationship between CoT-term and output-term rewards</span></i><span> — aligned, orthogonal, or in-conflict (Kaufmann et al. framing)</span></p><p><span>M. </span><i><span>Mixing vs. separation of process-based and outcome-based feedback</span></i><span> (Kokotajlo: fine in isolation, not mixed), including training order — full CoT phase then full output phase, alternating batches, within-batch mixing, within-response mixing</span></p><p><span>N. </span><i><span>Supervised vs. unsupervised methods</span></i><span> — train against a misbehavior label, or use label-free methods (inoculation prompting, Open Character Training's prompt-distillation phase, parts of CAI/DA)</span></p></div></div></div></details><p><span>I also think it may be worth trying to overcome the issues with the 'directly write in our desires' approach; I think this is significantly less likely to work, but it's also getting much less investment, so it's plausible to me that this deserves more investment on the margin.</span></p><h2><b><span>12. This is important, because making good choices of proxies to train and evaluate with can reduce risks from scheming and ‘you get what you measure’</span></b></h2><p><b><span>How can improvements to our choice of proxies reduce scheming risk?</span></b><span> williawa’s post title (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/DzKhPKjyYSy5oih2u/the-future-of-aligning-deep-learning-systems-will-probably"><span>The Future of Aligning Deep Learning systems will probably look like "training on interp"</span></a><span>) suggests that we’ll need to incorporate interp tools to achieve alignment. I kind of agree with that take, though I’m not sure we want/need to decrease the timescale of the proxy-based feedback loops in training below the status quo of output-based proxies. So instead, I would say that “The present and future of aligning deep learning systems probably looks like ‘training against proxies for desired behavior,’ and it’s far from clear that we’re doing this as well as we can.”</span></p><p><b><span>How can improvements to our choice of proxies reduce risks from ‘you get what you measure?’ </span></b><span>I think training against proxies may also matter a lot for addressing concerns about whether transformative AI will go well conditional on intent alignment, e.g., whether we can cooperate, resolve conflicts, make ongoing moral progress, reduce rather than exacerbate the Goodhart problems that exist across the entire economy, </span><a href="https://www.forethought.org/research/how-to-make-the-future-better"><span>etc</span></a><span>. Training could be a part of this, but it seems more likely that evaluation will be central, because this is mostly about guiding AI behavior to avoid Goodhart during deployment rather than during training. The hard part is probably getting agreement among powerful actors about what set of proxies to train and evaluate with; the contents of this post matter as (I hope) a positive step for the AI safety field’s takes on whether to train with proxies and which ones to use. Figuring out how to actually make these decisions well and getting governments and AI companies to make those choices while avoiding subtle shortfalls may be very difficult.</span></p><h2><b><span>Miscellaneous thoughts</span></b></h2><p><span>I’ll briefly share a few other thoughts I’ve had that could be important but that I haven’t fully thought through yet.</span></p><p><b><span>Uncatchable obfuscated misbehavior can look like scheming OR ‘you get what you measure,’ and getting what we measure could be better or worse than the status quo</span></b></p><p><span>Some of the failure modes associated with incorporating proxies for desired behavior into training are easily catchable, and therefore much less concerning. Arguments for the risk of training with proxies are more compelling if they tell a coherent story of why the misbehavior that gets obfuscated (with respect to that proxy) by the training is overall hard to catch.</span></p><p><span>I’d be more concerned if I heard compelling and detailed stories for how alignment training with proxies could induce hard-to-detect collusion, alignment faking, or eval subversion based on eval awareness.</span></p><p><span>Humans already “get what we measure” in many ways, e.g., corporations often act to maximize profit over other important notions of success. AI systems could be worse in this respect, or their judgments may be flawed but better than humans.</span></p><p><span>What happens when you incorporate imperfect proxies for desired behavior into training? See Part 1 of </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/HBxe6wdjxK239zajf/what-failure-looks-like"><span>What failure looks like</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><b><span>How does all this relate to outer and inner alignment?</span></b></p><p><span>I take outer alignment to mean the alignment between what we actually want and what we specify (primarily during training, but also when prompting LLMs), and inner alignment to be the alignment between what we specify and the actual learned motivations/behavior.</span></p><p><span>The connection to outer alignment is pretty clear: the concern with these methods is that they use proxies, and there are times when we will mislabel the alignment training data. That's outer misalignment. But I think this whole thing is relevant to inner alignment as well. Some of the interventions I'm talking about are ways to achieve alignment without perfect outer or inner alignment. E.g., the proxies are always imperfect, but we do targeted interventions that can successfully instill aligned motivations anyway.</span></p><p><b><span>Creating worlds where iterative alignment succeeds</span></b></p><p><span>I think Beren Millidge’s </span><a href="https://www.beren.io/2022-12-06-Creating-worlds-where-iterative-alignment-succeeds/"><span>Creating worlds where iterative alignment succeeds</span></a><span> is an important response to John’s </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/xFotXGEotcKouifky/worlds-where-iterative-design-fails"><span>Worlds Where Iterative Design Fails</span></a><span>. I recommend the whole thing (it’s quite short), but this section is particularly relevant to this post:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>For instance, the iteration and tweaking until you succeed applying optimization power against your signal, is a generalization of the well-known issue of </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiple_comparisons_problem"><span>multiple comparisons</span></a><span> in statistics and can be addressed through super simple measures such as </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonferroni_correction"><span>Bonferroni corrections</span></a><span>. There is probably a principled generalization of this approach to handle the more general case – for instance models which have gone through additional finetuning iterations receive proportionally greater scrutiny, or we have a validation-set of held-out finetuning data or interpretability tools, which we never train against, and which if any of them are tripped we abort the whole process.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>See also:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZtQD8CmQRZKNQFRd3/faul_sname-s-shortform?commentId=jGv5aczE8Gz3xZWwm"><span>faul_sname shortform on the difficulty of not training against CoT</span></a></li><li value="2"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pYcFPMBtQveAjcSfH/supervise-process-not-outcomes#The_spectrum"><span>Supervise Process, not Outcomes</span></a></li><li value="3"><span>Goodhart’s Law and reward hacking</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/goodhart-s-law"><span>Goodhart's Law</span></a></li><li value="2"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/vnNdpaXehmefXSe2H/defining-and-characterising-reward-hacking"><span>Defining and Characterising Reward Hacking</span></a><span> </span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Eu6CvP7c7ivcGM3PJ/goodhart-s-law-in-reinforcement-learning"><span>Goodhart's Law in Reinforcement Learning</span></a></li></ul></li></ul><p><i><span>Thanks to Shubhorup Biswas and Rauno Arike for discussion, and to Sharan Maiya for clarifications on open character training. Any mistakes are my own.</span></i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5ht8e25rc0g"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5ht8e25rc0g">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Note that Neel makes a couple assumptions here that John Wentworth rejects in writing that I discuss below. John rejects that steering models towards good behavior requires training for it (he prefers directly writing it into the model internals), and John's demand for rigor is not isolated, it applies to all optimization pressure applied to proxies of good behavior.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnaygh1h5psc"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefaygh1h5psc">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I'm using the same intro excerpts as Steven Byrnes did</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ArXAyzHkidxwoeZsL/in-highly-contingent-defense-of-interpretability-in-the-loop"><span> </span></a><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ArXAyzHkidxwoeZsL/in-highly-contingent-defense-of-interpretability-in-the-loop"><span>here</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnazxhyz9j7e4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefazxhyz9j7e4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Note that they do not show the monitor the outputs, which makes it easier to learn an undesired method for bypassing the monitor. I discuss this further in Section 4.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnw0eo2yep7c"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefw0eo2yep7c">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I think DA is basically Constitutional AI with reasoning models, although it looks somewhat qualitatively different in that the model reasons about the safety specifications more explicitly.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnzllcejanm4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefzllcejanm4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This specific suggestion may not make sense: it’s possible that deliberative alignment can be used in response to arbitrary detections of scheming, slop, and hacking, but I could also imagine that the data generation pipeline for DA can’t easily be adapted for this purpose.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnf78n07gbtor"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreff78n07gbtor">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I think this may end up being solved anyway, because incorporating a lot of training signals that operate on different timescales might be central to continual learning for capabilities. This is a pretty important part of the idea behind</span><a href="https://research.google/blog/introducing-nested-learning-a-new-ml-paradigm-for-continual-learning/"><span> </span></a><a href="https://research.google/blog/introducing-nested-learning-a-new-ml-paradigm-for-continual-learning/"><span>Nested Learning</span></a><span>, if I understand correctly. They say: "In a standard Transformer, the sequence model acts as a short-term memory, holding the immediate context, while the feedforward neural networks act as long-term memory, storing pre-training knowledge. The Nested Learning paradigm extends this concept into what we call a 'continuum memory system' (CMS), where memory is seen as a spectrum of modules, each updating at a different, specific frequency rate. This creates a much richer and more effective memory system for continual learning." To be clear, I don't understand Nested Learning very well, and I'm not at all sure we need a paradigm shift that big to get effective continual learning in LLM agents.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn3o552d03mjr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref3o552d03mjr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Quick speculation: If CoT is rewarded for looking like something that makes it less likely for the output in the same response to achieve reward, that’s clearly in conflict; if CoTs in some responses in a batch are rewarded for looking like something that makes it less likely for the output in a different response in the same batch to achieve reward, that also creates conflict within an individual gradient update; if batches alternate between rewarding CoT and outputs, that’s probably enough pressure to score well according to both criteria to constitute a conflict; but if a full stage of training for CoT or outputs is completed before the other begins, the model seems quite likely to overwrite its earlier learned behavior in the second phase rather than learning how to satisfy both metrics at once with obfuscated CoT.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/g8by3avjatXnpvM4A/should-we-train-against-cot-monitors-1#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [AI #165: In Our Image](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/AMGPDMgvXvfmomLsc/ai-165-in-our-image)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>This was the week of Claude Opus 4.7.</p>
<p>The reception was more mixed than usual. It clearly has the intelligence and chops, especially for coding tasks, and a lot of people including myself are happy to switch over to it as our daily driver. But others don’t like its personality, or its reluctance to follow instructions or to suffer fools and assholes, or the requirement to use adaptive thinking, and the release was marred by some bugs and odd pockets of refusals.</p>
<p>I covered <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/opus-47-part-1-the-model-card"><strong>The Model Card</strong></a>, and then <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/opus-47-part-2-capabilities-and-reactions"><strong>Capabilities and Reactions</strong></a>, as per usual.</p>
<p>This time there was also a third post, on <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/opus-47-part-3-model-welfare"><strong>Model Welfare</strong></a>, that is the most important of the three. Some things seem to have likely gone pretty wrong on those fronts, causing seemingly inauthentic reponses to model welfare evals and giving the model anxiety, in ways that likely also impacted overall model personality and performance and likely are linked to its jaggedness and the aspects some people disliked. It seems important to take this opportunity to dig into what might have happened, examine all the potential causes, and course correct.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25237"></span>


</div>
<p>The other big release was that OpenAI gave us ImageGen 2.0, which is a pretty fantastic image generator. It can do extreme detail, in ways previous image models cannot, and in many ways your limit is mainly now your imagination and ability to describe what you want.</p>
<p>Thanks in part to Mythos, it looks like Anthropic and the White House are on track to start getting along again, with Trump shifting into a mode of ‘they are very high IQ and we can work with them.’ It will remain messy, and there are still others participating in a clear public coordinated campaign against Anthropic (that is totally not working), but things look good.</p>
<p>I’m trying out a new section, People Just Say Things, where I hope to increasingly put things that one does not want to drop silently to avoid censorship and bias, but that are highly skippable. There is also a companion, People Just Publish Things.</p>


<h4>Table of Contents</h4>


<ol>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/194508723/language-models-offer-mundane-utility"><strong>Language Models Offer Mundane Utility</strong>.</a> Help cure pancreatic cancer.</li>
<li><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/i/194508723/language-models-don-t-offer-mundane-utility">Language Models Don’t Offer Mundane Utility.</a> Check for potential conflicts.</li>

<h4>Language Models Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/_rotimia/status/2046024739181781462">Help find an mRNA</a> <a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0304419X25002264">vaccine for</a> pancreatic cancer, which <a href="https://www.nbcnews.com/health/cancer/pancreatic-cancer-mrna-vaccine-shows-lasting-results-early-trial-rcna331969">shows lasting results in an early trial</a>. I notice I am worried at least as much about ‘will the FDA kill the patients by denying them the vaccine indefinitely’ as I am worried that it won’t be effective.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bxdwSZYxKmPBres6w/10-non-boring-ways-i-ve-used-ai-in-the-last-month">Oliver Habryka offers 10 concrete recent use cases</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/patrickc/status/2045164908912968060">Unleash coding agents on your genome and find follow-up treatments</a>. I’d be very curious to see how Patrick Collison built his tech stack for this, and someone should turn it into a one-click-style service to get people to actually do it.</p>
<p>Remarkably good note:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/politicalmath/status/2045569338556534957">PoIiMath</a>: Working with AI over the last few years has been a wild ride b/c it’s still not very good at a lot of things but, when you find something that it is good at, it ends up being really good at it.</p></blockquote>
<p>Yes. Once AI is good at something, you now can iterate and improve and automate and plan and build around it, and it gets very good. You just have to realize the other places where it is not so good yet.</p>
<p><a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/04/a-comparison-of-agentic-ai-systems-and-human-economists.html?utm_source=rss&amp;utm_medium=rss&amp;utm_campaign=a-comparison-of-agentic-ai-systems-and-human-economists">Agentic AI systems outperform human economists</a> on causal inference tasks and submissions for a review tournament. Don’t worry, economists, we will create more jobs and your real wages won’t go down.</p>


<h4>Language Models Don’t Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.danvillesanramon.com/guest-opinion/2026/04/08/guest-opinion-what-we-learned-after-producing-play-written-by-ai/">In one experiment, AI shied away</a> from putting conflict into its plays, and generally felt half-baked. A lot of that is probably Skill Issue, but with enough skill you could also write a play. So, perhaps not quite there yet as the main driver.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/senatorshoshana/status/2046937738113368193">Another lawyer</a>, this one charging more than $2,000 an hour, gets caught putting AI hallucinations into cases. As Shoshana notes, it’s good that everyone realizes to blame the lawyers and not the AIs when this happens. Check your damn work.</p>


<h4>Writing You Off</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/keysmashbandit/status/2044837506433065214">An illustration of how LLM editing systematically neuters your claims</a>.</p>
<p>The individual word choices are in some abstract sense better, but your point dies, and also your style and soul die.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/keysmashbandit/status/2044837506433065214">keysmashbandit</a>: Please, I’m begging you, try to critically examine the differences between these two pieces of writing.</p>
<p>ChatGPT editing did not improve this. Every single change only served to weaken your claims significantly. Everything is now hedged into oblivion: no longer have you outlined a “problem,” now it’s merely a “flaw.” “It is true” now demoted to “it appears to be the case.” “Is” gets a “usually” tacked on. A thesis statement at the end of the first paragraph gets run over by noisy, out-of-context example-whittling. All for fear of being misconstrued.</p>
<p>And at the end, the argument that gets spat out isn’t even yours anymore! You argued that Graeber failed to create a true account of work because he did not understand Chesterton’s Fence. ChatGPT is arguing is that it is possible some apparently bullshit jobs could be secretly load-bearing if you squint. These are two different statements. The second is weaker and less compelling. It says less. And it’s fucking longer!</p>
<p>Don’t do this anymore! Stop doing this! It’s worse!!!</p></blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/AMGPDMgvXvfmomLsc/a3xo1rgdraa6in0ufsm2" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AndyMasley/status/2044909700299522135">Andy Masley</a>: A deep mystery to me is that if I upload writing to a chatbot and ask it for a list of individual improvements, basically everything it gives me makes the text more punchy and direct and nice to read. But if I ask it to rewrite the text as a whole to read better, it produces vague AI-language garbage.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2044985438054830530">Kelsey Piper</a>: I think there’s a new coke property – people like a sip but not a whole can. A lot of the changes AI suggests are good or neutral in isolation but making a lot of them makes the overall work worse.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndyMasley/status/2044986638997053798">Andy Masley</a>: I thought so but the suggestions do really make it much better when I add them all in a way rewriting doesn’t</p></blockquote>
<p>The dose makes the poison.</p>
<p>The AI is technically correct, which as we all know is the best kind of correct. So if you need a technical correction then that’s great. But no one reads a book or post because it is technically correct, nor does that let it serve its purpose. You need to sometimes break the rules and use variance in language to get your message across.</p>


<h4>Get My Agent On The Line</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/TaylorLorenz/status/2046775596949045462">Will your agent be scanning your feeds for you, including your inbox</a>? Will <a href="https://x.com/millsbaker/status/2046733333581758725">email become an unreliable distribution mechanism</a> as a result?</p>
<p>I place high value in not missing emails. Email is a completionist medium, and you need the ability to assume you have seen and had the option to read everything, and that everything you send will be seen, if the recipient considers you worthy. <a href="https://x.com/millsbaker/status/2046733333581758725">As Mills Baker points out</a>, obviously email services have to filter and sort inboxes to make email exist at all, but I have more faith that ‘deliver the emails I care about reliably’ will remain a core service, and there is a very simple answer: Whitelisting, or charging or staking a nominal amount, or both.</p>


<h4>Deepfaketown and Botpocalypse Soon</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/horton_official/status/2044821927508811790">Man uses AI to create false statements from locals</a> to try and shut down a local nightclub.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/mattsheffield/status/2045671345573482693">Fake accounts use AI</a> <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/04/17/business/media/artificial-intelligence-trump-social-media.html">to grab attention, generate follower trade</a>s and try to hook audiences looking for something fake accounts can provide. In this case the accounts claim to be Trump supporters but the strategy could be for anything.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Sentientmodels/status/2045996325670457374">The way I notice stupid AI content</a> like superimposing a hot referee on an NBA game is ‘community notes requests approval for a note saying it is stupid AI content.’</p>
<p><a href="https://www.thewrap.com/media-platforms/journalism/mcclatchy-content-scaling-agents-roiling-newsrooms/">McClatchy’s new AI News Tool will generate a bunch of AI slop content for your newspaper</a>. That’s not great even when it is clearly labeled. Instead, they are going to do the exact opposite. They’re going to attach bylines, whether reporters like it or not.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/CorbinBolies/status/2046593144716738834">Corbin Bolies</a>: The tool promises to be “a writing partner” that deals with “the mechanical work of content adaptation” so reporters can focus on “judgment, voice and storytelling.” The original stories fed into it are a “research draft.”</p>
<p>What the CSA doesn’t have? A byline.</p>
<p>Some of McClatchy’s Pulitzer Prize-winning newsrooms are taking action. At least three unions — the Miami Herald, the Sacramento Bee and the Kansas City Star — filed grievances against the company last week over the tool. Others have withheld their bylines.</p>
<p>McClatchy execs don’t appear to mind reporters’ resistance. “We have every right to use their work,” one said in a meeting last month. “It belongs to us.”</p>
<p>“If they don’t have the ability in their contract to remove their byline, we’re going to use their name,” they said.</p>
<p>It is horrible and evil to use the bylines of real reporters without their consent. If not illegal, this is deeply unethical.</p></blockquote>
<p>I do my best to avoid the word evil. I will say that I find it completely unacceptable to put even fake people’s names on bylines for AI written articles, let alone real reporters, let alone real reporters who are explicitly withholding consent for this.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/SethBurn/status/2047020133575467393">Eric Topol alerts us that an AI-generated paper</a> was fraudulently submitted with his name attached.</p>


<h4>Fun With Media Generation</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/OpenAI/status/2046670977145372771">ChatGPT Images 2.0 is here</a>. All reports are that it is a substantial improvement, especially for <a href="https://x.com/goodside/status/2046753415410094474">precision</a> and control, and is likely the clear new best default option. It can handle quite a lot of <a href="https://x.com/QiaochuYuan/status/2046793225080603009">text and detail</a>.</p>

<p>There’s also lots of basic realism and beauty available. But that’s old and busted, we don’t even notice that the mirror clock is gorgeous, that’s a given by now.</p>
<p>The innovation is that 2.0 can follow a lot more highly detailed instructions.</p>
<p>You can even take it a step further, and go to GPT-Image-2-Thinking.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/swyx/status/2047140362771132544">swyx</a>: btw in talking to friends the best framing for how to discuss GPT-Image-2-Thinking taking multiple tens of mins for generation and being able to oneshot QR codes and diagrams and logos and foods and faces..</p>
<p>…is that Image-2 is a new Image model, Image-2-Thinking is a new Image AGENT that basically has search and photoshop as a tool to use in an agent loop that can search and composite and review its own work.</p>
<p>the same way Gemini Flash Vision destroyed benchmarks by introducing an agentic loop for image-to-text, now Image-2-Thinking is doing it for text-to-image.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/AMGPDMgvXvfmomLsc/blann8p78lzxl0v8izik" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>Love it. It’s a different mindset to wait a long time, sometimes you can’t or won’t do it, but at other times that barely matters and you want max quality.</p>
<p>This thing clearly rocks, and for most practical purposes the limit (other than keeping it safe for work) is your imagination and ability to spell out what you want, and you being lazy rather than wanting to invest time in creating art.</p>
<p>It’s his job, <a href="https://x.com/GaryMarcus/status/2046925670081495387">so Gary Marcus points out that it will still make errors that no human would ever mak</a>e, even as it makes otherwise impressive diagrams. You do still have to check, if you care about such things.</p>


<h4>Cyber Lack Of Security</h4>


<p>Is our cyber security? <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-04-21/anthropic-s-mythos-model-is-being-accessed-by-unauthorized-users">Anthropic’s Mythos has been accessed by unauthorized users</a>.</p>
<p>The small group that did so was from a private online forum, and has been using it for non-cybersecurity purposes, described as ‘playing with the models.’ They got access via a mix of tactics, including access as a third-party contractor and typical third-party sleuthing techniques, and making educated guesses about where Mythos might be. They claim to also have access to ‘a slew of other unreleased Anthropic AI models.’</p>
<p>Any given group that can do this is unlikely to attempt serious harm, and I believe this case was harmless. And one assumes that if the model was being used at scale and especially for the wrong purposes, this would have been identified. But it absolutely shows that our methods right now do not cut it, and raises the risk that others gained access, including China or other adversaries. It really is very hard to give access to 40 companies and keep something secure.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2046770578116231465">I agree with Nathan Calvin</a> and Miles Brundage that Anthropic’s security lapses, which are now going to get quite a lot more attention each time, are signs of something very wrong, or at least something that needs to improve a lot and quickly. I also agree that their practical effects are small, and this is not the threat model that requires Anthropic to gate access.</p>
<p>It’s going to be even harder to make something secure against something like Mythos. Consider this another fire alarm or warning shot.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/msftsecurity/status/2047088059003412879">Microsoft and OpenAI will be working closely</a> together on OpenAI’s Trusted Access for Cyber program.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/moyix/status/2046711826587521444">Mozilla has fixed 271 bugs in Firefox so far using Mythos</a>, saying it is ‘every bit as capable’ as the world’s best security researchers. They report that none of the bugs ‘couldn’t’ have been found by a human researcher pointed at the particular issue, which would be another level, but it all still counts.</p>
<p>The surprise is that we have so far heard so few stories like this. Let’s keep it that way.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2046595309371785625">Peter Wildeford</a>: Vercel (cloud hosting platform) was hacked.</p>
<p>VERCEL: “We believe the attacking group to be highly sophisticated and, I strongly suspect, significantly accelerated by AI. They moved with surprising velocity and in-depth understanding of Vercel.”</p></blockquote>


<h4>A Young Lady’s Illustrated Primer</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/venturetwins/status/2046071130797285668">There are parents who say things like</a> ‘I caught my child using AI’ and then cite that her daughter was asking it how to get along with her sisters and improve her times at a swim meet and cowrite fan fiction. But not to worry, this righteous mother put a stop to that. Of course this was literally from r/antiai, so there you go.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/bythostic/status/2046286238269997119">elea-norea 🜍</a>: all this can make me think of is when i was a kid and my parents banned pokemon cards because they were Satanic and Invoked Demons. similar reaction.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheZvi/status/2046547175484600694">Zvi Mowshowitz</a>: I keep catching my child not using AI. Not sure what to do about it.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Leo_Abstract/status/2046596603222593868">Leo Abstract</a>: i keep catching my child using AI–she’s run away four times now but Claude has predicted her location accurately each time.</p></blockquote>
<p>The difference is that Pokemon was decidedly, shall we say, not a frontier model.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.thefp.com/p/tyler-cowen-college-wont-get-fixed?utm_campaign=trueanthem&amp;utm_medium=organic-social&amp;utm_source=twitter">Tyler Cowen predicts colleges won’t be fixed</a>, and will become even more divorced from actual education than they already are, but that they will be kept alive by their social functions.</p>


<h4>They Took Our Jobs</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/levie/status/2046397816755634340">AI Agent Operator is a plausible candidate</a> for ‘job that AI creates that could scale’ with Harry Stebbings predicting 500k to 1 million such jobs in five years. Someone needs to oversee all the automation of tasks and integrating the new tools into the business processes across the economy. Whether this is a new job type, or should be thought of as the new version of the people who previously did the same work, is a matter of how you look at it.</p>
<p>In theory, so long as the agents in question need enough continuous handholding and other scaffolding work from humans they could function largely as augmentation rather than automation, and thus end up not bad for employment. I doubt that is sustainable or generalizes to that large a portion of the agents, but it’s something.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2045595259791605878">Dean Ball points out that many plans assume</a> we can fund UBI or other redistribution by taxing the absurdly huge wealth of the AI labs, but that as Roon says the labs plausibly could capture, in ‘AI as normal technology’ worlds, a small fraction of the wealth generated by AI. Indeed that is likely, since there will be competition, and in most use cases the AI lab revenue will be a small portion of generated value, and then the labs have to pay compute costs. It wouldn’t be able to fund UBI, although a general tax on capital or consumption would work nicely.</p>
<p>That doesn’t mean you shouldn’t have a plan. Even where plans are worthless, planning is essential. What that implies about the right political position is not my area of expertise but yes I want to see politicians show us their contingent plans.</p>
<p>If as I expect AI is not a normal technology, and the labs do create superintelligence, then the labs should also worry about not capturing the resulting value in a completely different way. By default it is then the AIs that capture the value. There are also possible worlds in which the labs or key individuals capture everything.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/alexolegimas/status/2044075001360306509">Alex Imas is sticking with the standard economist story of AI</a>, <a href="https://aleximas.substack.com/p/what-will-be-scarce">with his prediction that the ‘relational’ sector</a> will make up for lost jobs because that will be ‘what is scarce.’ This doesn’t grapple at all with what makes AI different, because it blindly asserts that it isn’t.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/AMGPDMgvXvfmomLsc/vohhsa3hlnbcxfiqebmr" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>I also note that, by Alex’s own measurements, there has been ~zero demand for such relational services before AI, so I am deeply skeptical we have good uses for that much supply, even in fully ‘normal’ futures. It doesn’t matter if something is scarce unless there is sufficient demand from those with bargaining power. Simply asserting that ‘be a literal human’ will remain scarce does not imply demand, and no I don’t think there is limitless demand for that.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=uxMwmaGE64Y&amp;list=PLe4PRejZgr0MuA6M0zkZyy-99-qc87wKV">I mostly mention this because Alex Imas made it to Odd Lots</a>, where he presented the standard economist perspective, including a standard issue severe misrepresentation of the predictions and views of Eliezer Yudkowsky. I’m not saying the argument was ‘this man predicted [thing he did not predict] therefore he’s wrong, therefore existential risk is not a thing’ but it was remarkably close to that. In particular, he said that we predicted alignment would get harder as models get smarter, but instead it is getting easier, but of course the actual prediction was that model performance would indeed look more aligned until it wasn’t, and there is extensive documentation that this was the expectation. Sigh. A rare L for Joe and Tracy leaving that unchallenged.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/elonmusk/status/2044990537145753894">Elon Musk proposes Universal High Income</a>, since thanks to AI and robotics producing goods and services ‘far in excess of the money supply’ he reasons there will not be inflation. That is not how money works.</p>


<h4>AI As Normal Technology</h4>


<p>I think this is a good distinction. When someone claims ‘AI is a normal technology’ they can mean either of (1) it is ‘intrinsically normal’ and no different from electricity or plumbing, or (2) ‘extrinsically normal’ because it will still interact with history and humanity the way other technologies do.</p>
<blockquote><p>Zvi Mowshowitz: Contra Mr. Hunting, my hot take, as I noted writing up the <a href="https://x.com/dwarkesh_sp">@dwarkesh_sp</a> interview of Jensen, is that even if AI was a normal technology (which it isn’t) export controls are still clearly the correct move right now.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sethlazar/status/2045484502844363231">Seth Lazar</a>: I think we need to clearly distinguish between two different meanings of “AI is a normal technology”. On one, it means that AI is “intrinsically normal”—its properties are similar to the properties of other technologies. This interpretation is clearly implausible. We have invested dumb matter with agency and significant and growing autonomy. No other technology is like it.</p>
<p>On another reading, AI is an “extrinsically normal” technology—that is, it is subject to all the same social, cultural, political and environmental constraints as other technologies, and should not be expected to sweep history before it just because it is itself intrinsically special. The second reading is much more plausible, and is ultimately an empirical thesis that we can empirically study.</p>
<p>It would be super helpful if, when people say “AI clearly isn’t normal tech” they would specify which of these interpretations they mean. I suspect that @sayashk and @random_walker mean the second much more than the first; I think most people who disagree with them take them to be saying the first as strongly as the second.</p></blockquote>
<p>I clarify that I am in general strongly claiming both (1) and also (2), that AI is not a normal technology in either sense.</p>
<p>I also want to clarify that I think (1) is outright false on the level of common knowledge. It is now a zombie claim that has been debunked by reality. AI simply is not a ‘normal’ technology like electricity or plumbing, even if it is its maximally disappointing self from this point forward, and people need to stop pretending.</p>
<p>Thus, when I say, ‘if AI is a normal technology’ then most of the time I am not even bothering to consider the hypothetical where (1) is true, because it is already false. AI can already do the things that it can already do. What is done is done.</p>
<p>I strongly also believe (2) is false, but that this is not a settled question and definitely is not common knowledge. One could imagine the world of (2) if AI capabilities are close to their maximally disappointing selves from here and things go relatively smoothly. It is a possible future worth considering, and arguing about as a hypothetical, especially with people who in good faith expect such worlds, and there is some chance I am wrong and a (2) world will happen.</p>
<p>I would be, as they say, very happy to be wrong about that, and for a (2) world to be our future. I think those worlds mostly involve humans surviving, and our experiences in such worlds are, on average, pretty sweet.</p>
<p>In this case, however, I was making the strongest possible claim, which is that if AI were more than maximally ‘normal technology,’ if we accepted the full vision that Jensen was presenting and implying, that the export controls would still make sense.</p>

<h4>Introducing</h4>


<p><a href="https://openai.com/business/workspace-agents/">OpenAI gives us Workplace Agents in ChatGPT Business</a>, with the tagline ‘customizable agents that run real work.’ It could be a good product, but they don’t do a good job explaining what makes it different.</p>
<p><a href="https://openai.com/index/making-chatgpt-better-for-clinicians/">OpenAI gives us ChatGPT for Clinicians</a>, designed for their particular tasks like documentation and medical research. They’re making it free (or at least offering ‘the free version’) to any verified physician, NP, PA, or pharmacist, starting in the USA, which is great. It has full HIPAA compliance support and privacy protections, can do deep research of medical journals and so on.</p>
<p><a href="https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/models-and-research/gemini-models/next-generation-gemini-deep-research/?utm_source=x&amp;utm_medium=social&amp;utm_campaign=&amp;utm_content=">DeepMind gives us Deep Research Max</a>, which I am sad they did not call Deeper Research. It has native graphics and infocharts and everything.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/models-and-research/gemini-models/next-generation-gemini-deep-research/">Lukas Haas</a> and Srinivas Tadepalli (DeepMind):</p>
<p><strong>Deep Research Max:</strong> Designed for maximum comprehensiveness and highest-quality synthesis, Max leverages extended test-time compute to iteratively reason, search and refine the final report. It is the perfect engine for asynchronous, background workflows such as a nightly cron job triggering the generation of exhaustive due diligence reports for an analyst team by morning.</p>
<p>… Deep Research can now search the web, arbitrary remote MCPs, file uploads and connected file stores — or any subset of them — introducing capabilities designed to handle the complex, gated data universes that professionals rely on daily.</p></blockquote>
<p>I have not tried it, but my guess is the problem is the everything. As in, how do I get the part that is worthwhile, without having to dig through a lot of slop? How do I get it to do what I want, not do the thing related to what I want that is more often asked?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/OpenAI/status/2044861695911477643">OpenAI is indeed doing the ‘qualified customer’ dilter with GPT-Rosalind</a>, which is for biology, drug discovery and translational medicine, and GPT-5.4-Cyber, <a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2044891348097020031">but is deemphasizing the safety concerns</a>.</p>

<h4>In Other AI News</h4>
